# THE DIAMOND SUTRA

<term>The Diamond Sutra</term> <note>original Sanskrit: Vajracchedikā Prajñāpāramitā Sūtra. The title translates to "The Perfection of Wisdom that Cuts like a Diamond." It refers to the text's ability to cut through worldly illusions and attachments to reveal the truth of reality.</note>

<summary>This title page identifies the manuscript as the Diamond Sutra, one of the most significant and influential scriptures in Mahayana Buddhism.</summary>
<keywords>Diamond Sutra, Vajracchedikā, Mahayana Buddhism, Wisdom, Scripture</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page serves as the introduction to the English edition, documenting the collaborative process between the translator and a friend that led from the study of Confucian and Taoist thought to the specific translation of the Diamond Sutra.</meta>

# PREFACE

THIS English version of *The Diamond Sutra*,¹ translated from the Chinese text of <term>Kumarajiva<note>A prolific translator-monk (344–413 CE) who was instrumental in bringing Buddhist scriptures from Sanskrit into the Chinese language.</note></term>, owes its inception to successive conversations with a friend, profoundly interested in the interpretation of oriental systems of philosophy. During those conversations renderings into English were made of numerous passages from the works of <term>Confucius<note>The Latinized name of Kong Fuzi, an influential Chinese philosopher whose teachings emphasized morality, social relationships, and justice.</note></term>, <term>Mencius<note>A philosopher (372–289 BCE) known as the "Second Sage" of Confucianism.</note></term>, and <term>Lao-Tsz<note>Also known as Laozi, the legendary founder of Taoism and author of the Tao Te Ching.</note></term>.

Having surveyed briefly those fertile fields of thought, we passed, by a natural transition, into the delectable Buddhist realm. Some passages from the *Chinese <term>Sutras<note>Derived from the Sanskrit word for "thread," a sutra refers to a scriptural narrative or religious discourse, specifically those regarded as the oral teachings of the Buddha.</note></term>*, comprising texts and annotations, were consecutively examined, and variously considered. Eventually it was suggested that *The Diamond Sutra*, perhaps one of the most <term>metaphysical<note>Referring to the branch of philosophy that examines the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter.</note></term> of the works ascribed to <term>Buddha<note>Siddhartha Gautama, the spiritual teacher and founder of Buddhism, who lived in ancient India.</note></term>, be conveniently rendered into the English language.

In order that the rather unfamiliar text might assume due intelligibility, parallel passages and numerous annotations were subjoined, as the pleasant work of translating proceeded. The idea of printing and publishing the text seemed to follow as a natural sequence.

¹ A learned Chinese commentator thus explained the rather striking title :
> “ As the diamond exceeds all other precious gems in brilliance and indestructibility ; so, also, does the wisdom of *The Diamond Sutra* transcend, and shall outlive, all other knowledge known to philosophy.”

<summary>This preface describes how the translation of the Diamond Sutra grew out of a broader exploration of Chinese philosophy and Buddhist scriptures. It explains that the title reflects the "indestructible" nature of the wisdom contained within the text.</summary>

<keywords>Diamond Sutra, Kumarajiva, Buddhism, Confucianism, Taoism, translation history, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, moving from the philosophical origins of the project to acknowledging existing scholarship and the Chinese monastic community that assisted in the translation.</meta>

# PREFACE

Already there exist in the English language renderings of *The Diamond Sutra* from the <term>Sanscrit<note>Also spelled Sanskrit; the ancient primary liturgical language of Hinduism and a philosophical language of Buddhism, Jainism, and Sikhism.</note></term> by <term>Max Müller<note>Friedrich Max Müller (1823–1900), a German-born philologist and one of the founders of the western academic field of Indian studies.</note></term>, and from the Chinese by <term>Beal<note>Samuel Beal (1825–1889), an English orientalist and the first person to translate the life of Buddha from Chinese into English.</note></term>. This new version does not seek to enter into rivalry with those erudite works; and a possible apology which might readily be offered for the publication of this modest volume is, that the scholarly productions of Müller and Beal, in their present forms, are perhaps slightly inaccessible to the general English reader.

It would appear that the peculiar charm of the Buddhist philosophy, and the remarkable purity of the Buddhist faith, are becoming more generally appreciated in Europe. Should this imperfect rendering of *The Diamond Sutra*, even in the faintest degree, confirm this just sense of appreciation, or prove a gentle incentive to further enquiry, then its unexpected publication may prove to be not entirely unjustified.

In recording our many obligations to those scholars whose works were frequently consulted, we also give expression to a hope that nothing of importance is omitted which ought to be gratefully acknowledged.

It may also be permissible to express admiration of the piety, and appreciation of the friendship, of those learned monks in Central China, to whom we are everlastingly indebted for even a slight initiation into those inexhaustible truths, which are alike the heritage, and the glory, of the disciples of <term>Buddha<note>The "Awakened One," specifically Siddhartha Gautama, whose teachings form the basis of Buddhism.</note></term>. Amongst those we should like to specify are Chang-Ming, the chief monk (<term>Seng-Kwan<note>original: "僧官" (sēng guān). A title for a government-appointed monastic official or supervisor of the Buddhist clergy.</note></term>) of <term>Chen-Chou<note>A historical prefecture in Hu-Nan province, China.</note></term> prefecture, <term>Hu-Nan<note>A province in south-central China.</note></term>, and the aged and affectionate Chioh-Hsien.

->WM. GEMMELL.<-

->POLLOKSHIELDS, GLASGOW,<-
->6th September 1912.<-

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, William Gemmell acknowledges previous translations by Max Müller and Samuel Beal while positioning his work as a more accessible version for the general public, ending with a tribute to the Chinese monks who guided his studies.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

The *Diamond Sutra* is one of the most valued and widely read philosophical works in <term>Buddhist literature<note>The body of religious and philosophical texts belonging to the Buddhist tradition, spanning various languages and centuries.</note></term>. It is very popular amongst ardent Buddhists in China, and excepting the *Lotus of the Good Law* <note>A translation of the *Saddharma Pundarika Sutra*, one of the most influential scriptures in East Asian Buddhism.</note>, and the *Leng-Yen-Ching*¹ <note>Also known as the *Shurangama Sutra*, a significant text in Chinese Zen (Chan) Buddhism regarding meditation and the mind.</note>, perhaps no other <term>Sutra<note>A Sanskrit word meaning 'string' or 'thread,' referring to a sermon or discourse attributed to the Buddha or his close disciples.</note></term> ascribed to <term>Buddha<note>Siddhartha Gautama, the spiritual teacher and founder of Buddhism, who lived in ancient India.</note></term> is regarded by the Chinese with so great esteem.

In Japan, *The Diamond Sutra* appears to be perused extensively by what <term>Max Müller<note>Friedrich Max Müller (1823–1900), a famous German philologist and orientalist who pioneered the study of comparative religion.</note></term> ² termed the **Shin-Gon** sect <note>Shingon Buddhism, a major school of Japanese Buddhism that emphasizes esoteric or 'secret' teachings.</note>, founded by **Ko-Bo** <note>Kūkai (774–835 AD), also known as Kōbō-Daishi, the monk who established Shingon Buddhism in Japan.</note>, a disciple of the renowned pilgrim **Hiuen-Tsang** <note>Xuanzang (602–664 AD), a famous Chinese monk who traveled to India to retrieve sacred texts and translate them into Chinese.</note>, about the year 816 A.D.

The *Diamond Sutra* was written originally in <term>Sanscrit<note>Also spelled Sanskrit; the primary liturgical and philosophical language of ancient India.</note></term>, and in process of time translated into the **Tibetan**, **Chinese**, **Mongol**, and **Manchu** languages. It represents the <term>Mahayana school<note>Literally the 'Great Vehicle,' one of the two main branches of Buddhism, which emphasizes the path of the Bodhisattva for the sake of all beings.</note></term> of Buddhist thought, a school founded by **Nagardjuna**³ <note>Nagarjuna (c. 150–250 AD), one of the most important Buddhist philosophers, known for his teachings on 'Emptiness.'</note> which flourished primarily at

---

¹ "The Sutra of firm establishment in all doctrine, describing clearly the secret merit and attainments in the religious life of <term>Tathagata<note>A title meaning 'One who has thus gone' or 'One who has thus come,' which the Buddha used when referring to himself.</note></term>." (Compare Edkins' *Chinese Buddhism*.)
² See the preface to *The Vagrakkhedika* <note>original: "Vajracchedika," the Sanskrit title of the Diamond Sutra, meaning "The Diamond Cutter."</note>.
³ "A native of Western India who lived as a hermit under an **Arguna** tree <note>The Terminalia arjuna, a large tree common in India.</note>, whence he derived his name. Converted by **Kapimala**, he laboured in Southern India as the fourteenth patriarch. . . . He is the chief representative, if not originator, of the Mahayana school, the"

Tchakuka <note>An ancient kingdom located in what is now the Yarkand region of Xinjiang, China.</note>, and thereafter influenced appreciably a considerable part of the Buddhist Church.

In the year 1836, Csomo Körösi <note>Alexander Csoma de Kőrös (1784–1842), a Hungarian philologist who compiled the first Tibetan-English dictionary.</note> published an account of the Tibetan translation, which interesting document may be consulted in Vol. XX. of the *Asiatic Researches*. *The Diamond Sutra* is therein designated "**The Sutra of Wonderful Effects**," a treatise by means of which Sakyamuni Buddha instructs Subhuti, one of his conspicuous disciples, in *The Prajna Paramita* <note>original: "Prajna Paramita"</note> or **transcendent wisdom**.¹

To Kumarajiva,² a native of Kashmir, who gained

greatest philosopher of the Buddhists, and as such styled "**one of the four suns which illuminate the world**." <note>original: "Tchatvara Suryas"</note> His own peculiar tenets have been perpetuated by a distinct metaphysical school called **Madhyamika** (<italic>Literally</italic> **The Middle Way** <note>original: "Juste Milieu"</note>), the characteristics of which are a sophistic nihilism which dissolves every proposition into a thesis and its antithesis, and denies both. "The soul," said Nagardjuna, "has neither existence nor non-existence, it is neither eternal nor non-eternal, neither annihilated by death nor non-annihilated." The tenets of this school are condensed in Nagardjuna's commentary on the *Mahaprajna Paramita S'astra* <note>The Great Treatise on the Perfection of Wisdom, a massive foundational text of Mahayana Buddhism.</note>. He spent the later part of his life in a monastery at Kosala . . . (correct date probably 194 A.D.). After his death he received the title **Bodhisattva** <note>An enlightened being who seeks to help all others reach enlightenment.</note>. He is the author of many **S'atras** <note>Philosophical treatises.</note>." (Compare Eitel's *Handbook of Chinese Buddhism*.)

¹ See the preface to *The Vagrakkhedika* <note>original: "Vagrakkhedika"; likely a variant spelling of *Vajracchedika*, the Sanskrit name for the Diamond Sutra, meaning "The Cutter that is like a Diamond."</note>.

² Kumarajiva was referred to as "**one of the four suns of Buddhism**" <note>original: "Tchatvara Suryas"</note>. He laboured in China as a most active and judicious translator, and is credited with having introduced a new alphabet. One of Kumarajiva's Chinese designations—**Tung-Sheo**—meant that, although young in years, he was ripe in the wisdom and virtues of old age. (Compare Eitel's *Handbook of Chinese Buddhism*.)

### INTRODUCTION

distinction as a monk of the later Chin dynasty^1 (A.D. 384-417), is conceded the honour of having first translated *The Diamond Sutra* into the Chinese language. Of subsequent Chinese translations, perhaps the most noteworthy is the text ascribed to the scholarly Xuanzang <note>The transcription uses the older spelling "Hiuen-Tsang" for this famous 7th-century Chinese Buddhist monk and traveler.</note>, and completed about the middle of the seventh century.^2

A rendering into English of Kumarajiva’s Chinese translation was accomplished by the Reverend Samuel Beal, and published in *The Journal of the Royal Asiatic Society*, 1864-65. The text and German translation of the Tibetan version were published in 1873 by Isaac Jacob Schmidt, in the *Memoirs of the Saint Petersburg Academy* <note>original: "Mémoires de l’Académie St Pétersbourg"</note>. The Mongolian translation was presented by the Baron de Constadt to the library of the Institute of France <note>original: "Institut de France"</note>. The Manchu translation is in the possession of Charles-Joseph de Harlez, who, with the aid of the Tibetan, Manchu, and Chinese versions, published a French translation of the Sanskrit text of *The Diamond Sutra* in the *Asiatic Journal* <note>original: "Journal Asiatique"</note>, 1892.^3 It has been observed^4 that “at first sight it may seem as

<page-num>xii</page-num>

### INTRODUCTION

if this metaphysical treatise <note>A formal piece of writing dealing with the nature of reality and existence.</note> hardly deserved the world-wide reputation which it has attained." Regarding this descriptive "world-wide reputation," devout Buddhists might suggest in extenuation <note>As a partial justification.</note>, that throughout many centuries, the "spiritual wisdom" of *The Diamond Sutra* produced in countless minds a "conscious blessedness of perfect peace." This "spiritual wisdom" also appeared to be a "strong incentive to holiness," and a grateful inspiration to those who had entered "the path which leads to Nirvana <note>The ultimate state of spiritual liberation and peace in Buddhism, signifying the end of suffering.</note>." In a few renowned monasteries of Central China, our Buddhist friends frequently affirmed that, by contemplating the "spiritual wisdom" of *The Diamond Sutra*, the mind would inevitably become "transfused with the mellow light of imperishable truth."

In the preface to *The Diamond Cutter* <note>original: "The Vagrakkhedika," the Sanskrit title meaning the "Diamond-Cutter" of ignorance.</note>, Max Müller <note>Friedrich Max Müller (1823–1900) was a famous German-born scholar who pioneered the study of Eastern religions in the West.</note> made a critical observation regarding certain peculiarities of "style" adopted in this treatise by the Buddhist philosophers who wished to convince their hearers of the truth of their philosophy. From the Sanskrit <note>The primary liturgical language of ancient Indian Buddhism.</note> text, perhaps it is difficult to realize fully what Ashvaghosha <note>original: "Asvaghocha," an influential Indian Buddhist philosopher and poet of the 2nd century.</note>¹ described as the "persuasiveness of Buddha's eloquence";² yet we may quite appreciate the academic instinct of Kumarajiva <note>The monk introduced on the previous page who first translated this sutra into Chinese around 400 AD.</note>, whose work on *The Diamond Sutra* bears evidence of a laudable endeavor to produce a classic, which in the Chinese language is almost entirely beyond reproach.

In all our aspirations to translate or to interpret Buddhist texts, perhaps it might prove advantageous to

<page-num>xiii</page-num>

### INTRODUCTION

bear in mind the significant words incorporated in the *Light of Asia* <note>An epic poem by Edwin Arnold (1879) that popularized the life of Buddha for English-speaking readers</note>:—

> " *And time hath blurred their script and ancient sense,*
> *Which once was new and mighty, moving all.* "

Max Müller <note>A pioneering 19th-century scholar of comparative religion</note> stated¹ that *The Diamond Sutra* <note>A fundamental text of Mahayana Buddhism focused on the nature of reality and non-attachment</note> represents a treatise on "metaphysical agnosticism," and he excused its "endless repetition of the same process of reasoning" on the assumption, that the subject-matter of the *Sutra* was probably "perfectly familiar to children and ignorant persons."

By referring to our Chinese text, we are led to suppose that *The Diamond Sutra* was "delivered expressly for those who had entered the Path which leads to Nirvana <note>The state of liberation from the cycle of suffering and rebirth</note>," and for those who are "attaining to the ultimate plane of Buddhic thought." Our Chinese annotators also appear to be unanimous in suggesting, that the "spiritual wisdom" of *The Diamond Sutra* is understood only in its rudimentary forms, by those of immature or uninitiated mind.

Concerning what has been termed the "agnosticism" of *The Diamond Sutra*, Sakyamuni Buddha <note>The historical Buddha, Siddhartha Gautama</note>, when he admissibly delivered the text, indicated clearly that there is a sense in which the "highest perfect knowledge"² may be referred to as "unknown." Dante appears to have had a similar difficulty regarding "knowledge" and "power" wherewith to express the higher forms of spiritual experience; and the following lines, constituting the opening stanzas of *The Paradiso* <note>The final part of Dante’s Divine Comedy, describing his journey through Heaven</note>, may serve to elucidate the Buddhist position.

---

1 In the preface to *The Diamond Cutter* <note>original: "The Vagrakkhedika," the Sanskrit title of the sutra</note>.
2 "Supreme spiritual wisdom." In Beal's translation of the *Diamond Sutra* <note>original: "Kin-Kong-King," the Chinese title</note>, "The unsurpassed, just, and enlightened heart." (Sanskrit: *The Heart of Unsurpassed, Right, and Complete Enlightenment* <note>original: "Annuttara Samyak Sambodhi Hridaya"</note>).

<summary>This page explores the interpretation of the Diamond Sutra, contrasting Max Müller's view of its repetitive style with traditional Chinese perspectives that see it as an advanced text for those seeking Nirvana. It also begins a comparison between Buddhist concepts of "unknowable" supreme knowledge and Dante’s struggles to describe the divine in The Paradiso.</summary>

<keywords>Max Müller, Diamond Sutra, Nirvana, spiritual wisdom, Sakyamuni Buddha, Dante, The Paradiso, The Diamond Cutter, enlightenment, metaphysical agnosticism</keywords>

->INTRODUCTION<-

and make it perhaps more intelligible to those who are as
yet unfamiliar with its peculiar modes of thought:—

> "The glory of Him who moves everything <note>original: *La gloria di colui che tutto move*</note>
> Penetrates through the universe, and shines
> More in one part, and less elsewhere.
> In the heaven that receives most of His light
> Was I; and I saw things which he who descends
> From there neither knows how nor is able to retell;
> Because, as it draws near to its desire,
> Our intellect¹ sinks so deep,
> That memory cannot follow after it." ²

In order to appreciate fully the philosophy of *The
Diamond Sutra* <note>A fundamental text of Mahayana Buddhism focused on the nature of reality and non-attachment</note>, doubtless it is necessary to interpret aright
the meaning of the Buddhist terminology. In this connec-
tion, the Sanskrit <term>Dharma</term> <note>A complex term meaning "the way things are," "the Law," "truth," or "phenomena"</note>—usually rendered into Chinese
by “Fah,” and into English by “Law”—appears to merit
our immediate attention.

Max Müller <note>A pioneering 19th-century scholar of comparative religion</note>, with his ample knowledge, stated that
Dharma, “in the ordinary Buddhist phraseology, may be
correctly rendered by Law; and thus the whole teaching of
Buddha is named *Saddharma* — ‘The Good Law.’ <note>original: *Saddharma*</note> What
*The Diamond Sutra* wishes to teach is that all objects,
differing one from the other by their Dharmas <note>In this context, referring to their individual characteristics or "qualities"</note>, are illusive,

# INTRODUCTION

...or as we should say, phenomenal and subjective, that they are, in fact, of our own making, the products of our own mind.” With those noteworthy observations, there is embodied in the preface to *The Diamond Sutra* <note>original: *Vagrakkhedika*, literally "The Diamond-Cutter"</note>, the following interesting suggestion: that the Greek **form** <note>original: εἶδος (eidos)—meaning whatever is seen, a shape, or a figure</note> appears to be the equivalent of the Sanskrit <term>Dharma</term> <note>A central term in Indian philosophy; in Buddhism, it can mean the cosmic law, the Buddha's teachings, or the fundamental elements of reality</note>.

Spence Hardy <note>A 19th-century missionary and scholar who studied Buddhism in Sri Lanka</note>, a distinguished writer on Buddhism, made a suggestion of perhaps equal importance, with reference to the correct interpretation of Dharma. In his well-known volume *Eastern Monachism*, there occurs the following relevant passage: “The second of the three great treasures <note>The "Three Jewels" or "Triple Gem": the Buddha, his Teachings (Dharma), and the Monastic Community (Sangha)</note> is called <term>Dharma</term> <note>original: Dhammo in Pali, or Dharmma in Sinhalese</note>. This word has various meanings, but is here to be understood in the sense of truth.”

Rhys Davids <note>An influential British scholar of the Pali language</note> in his useful volume *Buddhism*, indicated that “Dharma <note>original: Pali *Dhamma*</note> is not law, but that which underlies and includes the law — a word often most difficult to translate, but best rendered here by Truth and Righteousness.” ¹

# INTRODUCTION

as expressing adequately the Sanskrit <term>Dharma</term> <note>Dharma: a complex term signifying cosmic law, Buddhist teachings, or the nature of reality</note>, these familiar terms being obviously at his command, might have been utilized at pleasure. Like the cultured <term>Asvaghocha</term> <note>A significant Indian Buddhist philosopher and poet</note>, <term>Kumarajiva</term> <note>A prolific translator of Buddhist texts into Chinese</note> may have regarded the “nature” of the Law as “co-extensive with the illimitable ocean of being”;^1^ and within that ample compass, perhaps he thought there might synthetically be included those beautifully-defined concepts “form,” “truth,” and “righteousness.”

Chinese annotators of <term>*The Diamond Sutra*</term> seldom criticize adversely its classic terminology, or suggest many inapplicable alternative renderings. They appear to have surveyed the realm of “spiritual wisdom” enunciated by <term>Sakyamuni Buddha</term> <note>The historical Buddha</note>, and thereafter to have become greatly impressed by the thought that, in its *Essence*, it might possibly be inexhaustible. This may in part explain their motive for incorporating in the commentary a familiar passage from <term>Lao-Tsz</term> <note>The founder of Taoism</note>, “Infinite truth is inexpressible”^2^—which in a measure illustrates the appreciable difficulty of stating, in exact terms of philosophy, the equivalent of the Buddhic “Law.”

In our intercourse with Buddhist monks, we heard the rather engaging suggestion, that the familiar Christian phrase, “the law of the spirit of life,” contains a spiritual concept which appears to approximate closely to the idea of the “Law” of Buddha. Those monks seemed to believe that the “Law”^3^ enters quietly and operates

# INTRODUCTION

<p-num>xviii</p-num>

The Sanskrit term <term>Samgna</term> <note>original: *Samjñā*; in Buddhist philosophy, this refers to perception, the faculty of recognizing and labeling things</note>, usually rendered into Chinese by <term>Ming</term> <note>original: 名 (*míng*), meaning "Name"</note>, and into English by “Name,” seems to deserve our further attention. Like the term <term>Dharma</term> <note>The cosmic law, truth, or the teachings of the Buddha</note>, a clear knowledge of “Samgna” is indispensable for a correct understanding of our text.

In one of the opening passages of *The Diamond Sutra*, we find that <term>Sakyamuni Buddha</term> <note>The historical Buddha, Siddhartha Gautama</note>, in reply to an enquiry by <term>Subhuti</term> <note>One of the Buddha's senior disciples, known for his deep understanding of "Emptiness"</note>, suggests that by means of this “wisdom,” enlightened disciples shall be enabled to bring into subjection every inordinate desire.

> “*Every species of life, whether hatched in the egg, formed in the womb, evolved from spawn, produced by metamorphosis, with or without form or intelligence, possessing or devoid of natural instinct—from these changeful conditions of being* <note>The text here transitions from a description of all living beings to the Buddhist goal of liberating them from the cycle of birth and death</note>

before the minds of his disciples in the simile of a “raft”—a thing to be abandoned when the mind “touched the further shore” of everlasting truth. It seems to be in this tentative sense that intellectual Buddhists regard all ecclesiastical institutions, priesthoods, dogmas, ordinances, etc.; and we have met monks who would classify “belief in the ‘efficacy’ of religious rites or ceremonies” with obnoxious forms of “heresy” and “immorality.” (Compare Rhys Davids’ *Buddhism* <note>T.W. Rhys Davids was a prominent 19th-century British scholar of the Pali language and founder of the Pali Text Society</note>.) With regard to the Buddhist objection concerning the “efficacy” of religious “rites,” compare the noble sentiments expressed in the following lines, delightfully rendered by Sir Edwin Arnold from the <term>Bhagavad-Gita</term> <note>A 700-verse Hindu scripture that is part of the epic Mahabharata</note> (*The Song Celestial*):—

> “Serenity of soul, benignity,
> Sway of the silent spirit, constant stress
> To sanctify the nature,—these things make
> Good rite, and true religiousness of mind.”

# INTRODUCTION

->xix<-

“*I command you to seek deliverance in the transcendental concept of <term>Nirvana</term> <note>Sanskrit: Nirvāṇa; the ultimate state of spiritual liberation and the extinction of suffering, desire, and the sense of self</note>. Thus you shall obtain deliverance from the idea of an immeasurable, innumerable, and illimitable world of sentient life; but, in reality there is no idea of a world of sentient life from which to obtain deliverance. And why? Because, in the mind of an enlightened disciple, there have ceased to exist such arbitrary ideas of phenomena as an entity, a being, a living being, or a personality.*”

A similar process of reasoning appears to permeate the whole of *The Diamond Sutra*, and whether appertaining to a living being,¹ a virtue,² a condition of mind,³ a Buddhist kingdom,⁴ or a personal Buddha,⁵ there is implied in each concept a spiritual essence, only imperfectly described, if not entirely overlooked, in the ordinary use of each particular name. Shakespeare enquired, “What’s in a name?” and in a thought inspired by the rose and its delicious fragrance, suggested with Buddha, that there is little, or nothing, in a name which explains the real nature of an object. Even a “particle of dust” seems, to the Buddhist mind, to embody in its composition a subtle spiritual element, entirely “inscrutable,” and quite “incomprehensible.”

According to the <term>Mahayana School</term> <note>Sanskrit: Mahāyāna, meaning "Great Vehicle"; a major branch of Buddhism that emphasizes the path of the Bodhisattva and the emptiness of all phenomena</note> of Buddhist thought, objects and their respective names are alike unreal and illusory. Objects and names, in the abstract, represent merely the products of untutored and unenlightened minds. Nothing is real, in the sense that it is permanent. Everything appears to be subject to irrevocable Laws of change and decay. As the things which we see are temporal, it

Many minds are susceptible of deception by the fleeting phenomena of life; but behind these phenomena there is an essential element, entirely spiritual <note>Some modern Japanese Buddhists appear to regard this purely spiritual element as the "essence of mind."</note>, uninfluenced by arbitrary ideas or changeful conditions, which "pervades all things," and is "pure" and "unchanging."

Perhaps it might prove of interest to quote the following outline of the <term>Great Vehicle</term> <note>Sanskrit: Mahāyāna; the branch of Buddhism concerned with the salvation of all beings and the concept of emptiness</note> doctrine <note>This excerpt is from the preface to *The Diamond Cutter* (Sanskrit: *Vajracchedikā*).</note> prepared by Mr. S. > "All things that are produced by causes and conditions are inevitably destined to extinction. There is nothing that has any reality; when conditions come things begin to appear, when conditions cease these things likewise cease to exist. Like the foam of the water, like the lightning flash <note>Compare to the similar metaphor found on page 110.</note>, and like the floating, swiftly vanishing clouds, they are only of momentary duration. As all things have no constant nature of their own, so there is no actuality in pure and impure, rough and fine, large and small, far and near, knowable and unknowable, etc. On this account it is sometimes said that all things are nothing. The apparent phenomena around us are, however, produced by mental operations within us, and thus distinctions are established. . . .

> “Though there are thus two kinds, perceptible and imperceptible phenomena, they occur upon the same things, and are inseparably bound together even in the smallest particle. Their difference in appearance is caused only by differences, both in mental phenomena and in the depth of conviction. Those who know only the perceptible things, without knowing the imperceptible, are called the unenlightened by <term>Buddha</term> <note>Sanskrit: "The Awakened One." In this context, it refers to the historical Siddhartha Gautama who achieved a state of perfect clarity regarding the nature of existence.</note>. . . .

> “In contradistinction to the fallacious phenomena, there is the true <term>Essence of Mind</term> <note>Often referred to in Sanskrit as *Bhutatathata* or "Suchness," representing the absolute reality that remains when all temporary delusions and labels are removed.</note>. Underlying the phenomena of mind, there is an unchanging principle which we call essence of mind. . . . The essence of mind is the entity without ideas and without phenomena, and is always the same. It pervades all things, and is pure and unchanging. . . . The essence and the phenomena of mind are inseparable; and as the former is all-pervading and ever-existing, so the phenomena occur everywhere and continually, wherever suitable conditions accompany it. Thus the perceptible and imperceptible phenomena are manifestations of the essence of mind that, according to the number and nature of conditions, develop without restraint. All things in the universe, therefore, are mind itself.
> 
> “By this we do not mean that all things combine into a mental unity called mind, nor that all things are emanations from it, but that, without changing their places or appearance, they are mind itself everywhere. Buddha saw this truth and said that the whole universe was his own. <note>This reflects the Mahayana concept of the *Dharmakaya*, the "Truth Body" of the Buddha, which is identical with the ultimate nature of the universe.</note> Hence it is clear that where the essence of mind

**Key Terms:**
*   **Perceptible phenomena:** The visible, audible world we experience through our senses.
*   **Imperceptible phenomena:** Subtle realities or causal forces that exist beyond immediate human sensory detection.
*   **Essence of Mind:** The fundamental, pure nature of consciousness that exists beneath our thoughts and perceptions.
*   **Unenlightened:** A state of being where one mistakes temporary appearances for ultimate reality.
*   **Mental unity:** The philosophical idea that all distinct objects are actually part of a single, underlying consciousness.

It explains that enlightenment consists of seeing beyond the surface appearance of things to recognize this universal mental nature.</summary>

is found, and the necessary conditions accompany it, the phenomena of mind never fail to appear. . . . Though there is a distinction between the essence and the phenomena of mind, yet they are nothing but one and the same substance, that is, ***mind*** <note>In this context, the author refers to the Mahayana doctrine of "Mind-Only" (*Vijnapti-matra*), which posits that the external world is a projection of consciousness.</note>. So we say that there exists nothing but mind. Though both the world of the pure and impure, and the generation of all things, are very wide and deep, yet they owe their existence to our mind."

Perhaps we might appropriately indicate that however interesting, or even fascinating, may be the nice distinction between ***mind*** and ***essence of mind***, in relation to phenomena, so far as we are aware, the distinction may be implied, but is never precisely stated, in the text of ***The Diamond Sutra***. Nevertheless, we may readily appreciate the subtle intellectual movement, which endeavours to distinguish clearly between the phenomena of mind, and an unchanging principle underlying it, capable of being defined as <term>Essence of Mind</term> <note>Sanskrit: *Bhutatathata*, often translated as "Suchness" or "Absolute Reality." It is the pure, distorted-free nature of reality that remains when all false thoughts are stilled.</note>. Yet we have a notion that our Japanese Buddhist friends intuitively find in their beautiful concept, infinitely more of a purely spiritual nature, than they attempt to express by the mere metaphysical term. Doubtless they have frequently applied to it the incisive logic of <term>Sakyamuni Buddha</term> <note>The "Sage of the Sakya Clan," the historical Buddha (Siddhartha Gautama).</note>, and found simultaneously, that what is ordinarily referred to as "essence of mind," is not in reality "essence of mind," it is merely termed "essence of mind." <note>This "Formula of Refutation" (A is not A, therefore it is called A) is the central logical tool of the Diamond Sutra. It is used to prevent the reader from clinging to any concept—even spiritual ones—as a fixed or permanent "thing."</note>

---

<note>1 Compare the process of reasoning which permeates the entire *Diamond Sutra*. We hope no injustice is done to our Japanese friends, by applying to their beautiful concept "essence of mind," this familiar logical method of Sakyamuni Buddha.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the philosophical argument that all reality is a manifestation of the mind. The author introduces the Diamond Sutra's signature logical style, which asserts that even the most sacred concepts are merely conventional labels and not absolute realities.</summary>

# INTRODUCTION

->xxiv<-

The term <term>Buddha</term> <note>Literally "The Awakened One." In Buddhism, it refers to one who has achieved a state of perfect enlightenment and can see the true nature of reality.</note>, as defined in *The Diamond Sutra*, seems to merit a brief consideration. In fulfillment of our present purpose, it seems almost unnecessary to enter into questions regarding the historical Buddha, or to the authenticity of Sutras ascribed to his genius. Therefore, without indicating any particular reservation, we meantime accept the traditional statements that the Buddha of *The Diamond Sutra* was the son of <term>Suddhodana</term>, the husband of <term>Yasodhara</term>, and the father of <term>Rahula</term> <note>These names refer to the family of Siddhartha Gautama, the historical prince who lived in India roughly 2,500 years ago and became the Buddha.</note>. But, incorporated with the text, there is embodied in the familiar term Buddha, a lofty spiritual concept, which seems to place it in a category where fresh interest is imparted to the question of its interpretation.

Concluding the twenty-sixth chapter of *The Diamond Sutra*, wherein “the spiritual body <note>original: *Fah-Shen* (法身)—the Law, or spiritual body. In Buddhist philosophy, this is the *Dharmakaya*, the ultimate reality beyond form. The author compares this to *Shen-Shen*, the term usually employed in the Chinese translation of the New Testament to denote the spiritual body.</note> is entirely differentiated from external phenomena,” <term>Sakyamuni</term> <note>A common title for the historical Buddha, meaning "The Sage of the Shakyas" (his clan).</note>, in reply to an enquiry regarding the possibility of perceiving “Buddha” by means of his bodily distinctions, delivered the following remarkable <term>Gatha</term> <note>original: *gāthā*. A verse or hymn used in Buddhist scripture, typically consisting of four lines.</note> :—

> *I am not to be perceived by means of any visible form,*
> *Nor sought after by means of any audible sound ;*
> *Whosoever walks in the way of iniquity,*
> *Cannot perceive the blessedness of the Lord Buddha.* <note>The author notes the similarity to lines from *The Song Celestial* (Sir Edwin Arnold’s 1885 translation of the *Bhagavad Gita*): "I am not known / To evil-doers, . . . nor to those / Whose mind is cheated by the show of things."</note>

# INTRODUCTION

->xxv<-

In the twenty-ninth chapter of *The Diamond Sutra*, wherein is expounded “the majesty of the absolute,” <term>Sakyamuni</term> <note>A title for the historical Buddha, meaning "Sage of the Shakya Clan."</note> declared that a disciple who affirms that “Buddha” comes or goes, obviously has not understood the meaning of his instruction. Because, as we learn from our text, the idea “Buddha” implies neither coming from anywhere, nor going to anywhere. This purely spiritual concept of Buddha seems to have seized the imagination, and inspired the writer of the *Yuen-Chioh Sutra*,<margin>In Buddhist terminology, *Yuen-Chioh* refers to the study of primary spiritual causes by means of contemplation or meditation.</margin> to whom are ascribed the following significant lines:

> “Like drifting clouds, like the waning moon, like ships that sail the ocean, like shores that are washed away—these are symbolic of endless change. But the blessed Buddha, in his essential, absolute nature, is changeless and everlasting.”

Again, in the seventeenth chapter of *The Diamond Sutra*, it is declared that in the word “Buddha,” every <term>Law</term> <note>In this context, "Law" refers to *Dharma*—the universal truths and natural laws that govern existence and spiritual path.</note> is intelligibly comprehended.<margin>Compare Samuel Beal’s rendering in the *Kin-Kong-King* <note>The Chinese title for the Diamond Sutra (*Jin gang jing*).</note>: “<term>Tathagata</term> <note>original: *Tathagata*. A title the Buddha used for himself, meaning "The Thus-Come One" or "One who has arrived at Truth."</note> is the explanation as it were of all systems of Law.” See also *The Book of the Manifesting of the One and Manifold* in *The Song Celestial* <note>Sir Edwin Arnold's famous 1885 poetic translation of the Bhagavad Gita, a sacred Hindu text.</note>, the verse commencing:</margin> To Western minds, it might become necessary to resist a natural inclination to ascribe to those elements of thought, an influence which had its

->“Thou, of all souls the Soul!<-
->The comprehending whole!”<-

In conversation with Chinese monks regarding the meaning of this impressive passage, we found that they invariably approved of a suggested rendering, that “Buddha is the *One* in whom all Laws become intelligible.”

# INTRODUCTION

->xxvii<-

reference is made by <term>Sakyamuni Buddha</term> <note>The historical Buddha, born Siddhartha Gautama, who lived in northern India approximately 2,500 years ago.</note> to the doctrines of <term>Karma</term> <note>The spiritual principle of cause and effect where intent and actions influence the future of that individual.</note> and Reincarnation. It seems to be an old truth to which expression is given in the *Epistle to the Galatians*: “Whatsoever a man soweth, that shall he also reap. For he that soweth to his flesh shall of the flesh reap corruption; but he that soweth to the Spirit shall of the Spirit reap life everlasting.” ¹

To the Buddhist mind, Karma is indissolubly associated with “the Law which moves to Righteousness.” Thus it is accustomed to view the traditional Christian idea of “justification by Faith,” rather as a devoutly-conceived theory, than as a reasonably-constructed truth.

Occasionally we have heard a gentle affirmation, that the Western mind seems unwittingly inclined to confound the doctrine of Karma with a concept which is almost suggestive of Fatalism. If Karma contains even a germ of thought which corresponds to “blind fatalism,” the idea is perhaps quite felicitously expressed in the following sentences, culled from a valued letter written by an aged Chinese monk: “Karma is a universal Law which gently binds us to the rhythmic cycle of evolving life. ¹ <term>Rhys Davids</term> <note>Thomas William Rhys Davids (1843–1922), a foundational British scholar of Pali and Buddhist studies whose work helped introduce Buddhism to the English-speaking world.</note>, when he expounded the doctrine of Karma in *Buddhism*, clearly indicated the Buddhist position, “that whatever a man reaps, that he must also have sown.” Chinese Buddhists appear to be assured, “that if a man reaps sorrow, disappointment, pain, he himself, and no other, must at some time have sown folly, error, sin; and if not in this life, then in some former birth. Where then, in the latter case, is the identity between him who sows and him who reaps? *In that which alone remains when a man dies, and the constituent parts of the sentient being are dissolved; in the result, namely, of his action, speech, and thought, in his good or evil Karma (literally his ‘doing’ <note>The word Karma is derived from the Sanskrit root 'kṛ', meaning 'to do' or 'to make'.</note>) which does not die.*”

# INTRODUCTION

->xxviii<-

operates so quietly and imperceptibly that we scarcely are conscious of its presence. The absolute truth of <term>Karma</term> <note>The spiritual principle of cause and effect; the sum of a person's actions in this and previous states of existence, viewed as deciding their fate in future existences.</note> greatly attracts our minds, which approve naturally of its consummate justice and perfect righteousness.”

Those ideas of “consummate justice” and “perfect righteousness,” seem to be faithfully portrayed in the following quotation, gleaned from *The Light of Asia*:—

> “ *What has been brings what shall be, and is,*
> *Worse—better—last for first and first for last:*
> *The Angels in the Heavens of Gladness reap*
> *Fruits of a holy past.*”
<note>original: "What hath been bringeth..." Quoted from Sir Edwin Arnold’s 1879 epic poem, which popularized the life of the Buddha for Western readers.</note>

It would therefore appear that Karma may be regarded generally, as comprising the constituent moral elements derived consecutively from the thoughts, words, and actions of an interminable life’s cycle. Perhaps it is in this connection that Chinese Buddhists frequently assume Karma to resemble “a moral fiber, indissolubly entwined in sentient life.” It may be believed to recede far into the past, and to extend indefinitely into the future.

Although realizing the significance of Karma,¹ the devout Buddhist mind is not usually disturbed by fearful forebodings. Ostensibly, it has evolved to a condition of holiness, wherein “the dross of sin” is entirely consumed in the “white flames” of <term>Sakyamuni’s</term> <note>A title of the historical Buddha, meaning "the Sage of the Sakya clan."</note> “transcendent wisdom” and “boundless love.”

In the text of *The Diamond Sutra*, it may be observed that <term>Sakyamuni Buddha</term> <note>A title of the historical Buddha, Siddhartha Gautama, meaning "Sage of the Shakya Clan."</note>, in discoursing to <term>Subhuti</term> <note>One of the Buddha's primary disciples, known for his understanding of "Emptiness" (Sunyata); he is the Buddha's primary interlocutor in the Diamond Sutra.</note>, referred incidentally to personal reminiscences, one of which belonged to a distant period of five hundred incarnations.

According to the text of *The Light of Asia* <note>An 1879 epic poem by Sir Edwin Arnold that popularized the life and teachings of the Buddha for the Victorian public.</note>, the spiritual consciousness of Sakyamuni Buddha extended to a period even more remote, as may be judged by these remarkable lines :—

> “ I now remember, myriad rains ago,
> What time I roamed <term>Himâla’s</term> <note>The Himalayas.</note> hanging woods.”

In considering briefly the doctrine of reincarnation, perhaps it might readily be conceded to our Buddhist friends, that there were exemplified in the Founder of their faith, a wonderful potency of intellect, and a marvellous degree of spiritual intuition. Quite agreeable, also, may be the suggestion, that this potency of intellect might become intensified, and probably “rendered subjective,” by “ascetic exercises,” abstract contemplation, and “determined effort.”

# INTRODUCTION

->xxx<-

If the human mind is likely capable of being connected to the <term>Divine Spirit</term> <note>In the context of 19th and early 20th-century scholarship, this often refers to a universal consciousness or the 'Atman' (Self) found in Indian philosophy.</note>, then the way of approach to an understanding of the Buddhist theory of <term>intuition</term> <note>Direct perception or knowledge of truth that occurs without the need for conscious reasoning.</note> becomes, perhaps, tolerably clear. Concrete knowledge acquired by intuition appears to assure our Buddhist friends of the *fact* of reincarnation. But they invariably refrain from a vain attempt to *prove* the "fact" by an authorized—and consequently stereotyped—process of reasoning.

The unknown Indian author of the <term>Bhagavad-Gita</term> <note>A 700-verse Hindu scripture that is part of the epic Mahabharata; it is a dialogue between the prince Arjuna and the deity Krishna.</note> revealed in simple phraseology the native idea of reincarnation; and suggested, happily, an instructive theory concerning the advent of great Teachers and Saviours in every age. > *... When Righteousness*

> *Is strong, I rise, from age to age, and take*
> *Visible shape, and move a man with men,*
> *Succouring the good, thrusting the evil back,*
> *And setting Virtue on her seat again.* "

> *To see one changeless Life in all the Lives,*

> *The separate existences apart,*
> *And, being separated, holds them real.*”

> “ *If any teach Nirvana is to cease,*
> *Say unto such they lie.*”

> “If any teach Nirvana is to live,

<note>This verse, likely from Edwin Arnold’s *The Light of Asia*, describes Nirvana not as a continuation of life as we know it, but as a state transcending the "broken lamps" of individual existence.</note>

In concluding, it might be opportune to observe, that the **value-judgments** <note>original: "Werthurtheile"</note>,¹ known amongst modern theologians as characterising the teaching of Albrecht Ritschl <note>Albrecht Ritschl (1822–1889) was a prominent German theologian who argued that religious knowledge consists of "value-judgments"—the moral and spiritual impact of a belief—rather than scientific or metaphysical facts.</note>—sounds, upon intimate acquaintance, merely as a faint echo of the logic of Sakyamuni Buddha. Ritschl might apply his **value-judgments** <note>original: "Werthurtheile"</note> to the presumed interpretation of a “miracle,” etc. Buddha suggested by his “method,” that what is ordinarily referred to as a “miracle,” is not in reality a “miracle,” therefore it is merely defined as a “miracle.” <note>This "method" refers to the core logic of the *Diamond Sutra*, which uses a three-part formula: a concept is stated, it is denied as a permanent reality, and it is then affirmed only as a conventional name. This prevents the mind from clinging to any fixed idea.</note> So, also, with the various dogmas which distinguish every religious creed. By many Chinese it is regarded as an evidence of Divinity, that in the mind of Sakyamuni Buddha there was conceived this incisive logical method ; and amongst the learned monks, profound homage is rendered, and much wonder expressed, because the Lord Buddha ² did not hesitate to apply its principles to every doctrine synonymous with his own accredited “Law.”

### THE DIAMOND SUTRA

---

<note>1. By adopting the term **Mieh-Tu** <note>A Chinese Buddhist term for "Deliverance" or "Salvation."</note>, Chinese Buddhists appear well-prepared to refute a common misunderstanding that their concept of deliverance is the same as total destruction. While **Mieh** usually signifies annihilation, **Tu** means to cross over in safety, which is the direct opposite of annihilation. After carefully considering the meaning of these terms, it should generally be accepted that translating **Mieh-Tu** as "Deliverance" or "Salvation" is quite justified.</note>

The poet Sir Edwin Arnold in *The Light of Asia* describes this state beautifully: "The dewdrop slips into the shining sea." The Chinese monk Chioh-Hsien offers a similar perspective: "The dewdrop becomes the shining sea once more." Most public religious systems define Nirvana in two ways. Negatively, it is a state of being completely exempt from the cycle of rebirth <term>transmigration: the soul's journey through successive lives</term>, and entirely free from physical matter, passion, and effort—a state of calm indifference to both joy and pain. Positively, it is defined as the highest stage of spiritual freedom and bliss; a form of absolute immortality where the soul is absorbed into itself. Even in this state, individuality is preserved, and Buddhas who have entered Nirvana may occasionally reappear to help those who remain faithful.</note>. # THE DIAMOND SUTRA

Two specific terms in Buddhist philosophy are used to label it: <term>the heresy of individuality <note>original: Sakkayaditthi</note></term> and <term>the doctrine of self <note>original: Attavada</note></term>. See footnote below.</note>

Buddhist psychology is made tolerably clear in the familiar narrative known generally as *The Enlightenment of Ananda* <note>Ananda was the Buddha’s cousin and closest disciple, known for his devotion and for memorizing all of the Buddha's sermons.</note>. Therein the Lord Buddha endeavors to prove that as objects within ourselves are invisible <note>meaning we cannot see our own internal organs with our eyes</note>, the illuminating mind cannot be asserted to inhabit exclusively our material bodies. He also indicates that it cannot be affirmed to occupy any appointed sphere outside ourselves, it being usually understood that we observe only those objects by which we are environed <note>surrounded</note>. The Lord Buddha also controverts <note>disputes or denies</note> the theory, enunciated by Ananda, that the mind is secreted somewhere within the organs of sense; which assumption is based upon a notion that the seeing eye, and differentiating mind, are mysteriously correlated.

...such as sound, scent, taste, touch, or the <term>Law <note>original: Fah / Dharma; here referring to the mind's objects or religious teachings</note></term>.¹ Subhuti, it is vital that an enlightened disciple, in the practice of <term>charity <note>original: Dana; the act of selfless giving</note></term>, should act independently of sensory phenomena. And why? Because by acting without regard for the deceptive forms of phenomena, he will realize a merit through his charity that is inestimable and immeasurable.

---

<note>1. <term>The Law <note>original: Fah, the Chinese equivalent of the Sanskrit term Dharma</note></term> appears to be a general term for all religious doctrines associated with Buddhism. The Buddhas are consistently referred to as "Princes of the Law" <note>original: Fah-Wang</note>. The Sutras (scriptures) are frequently called "Jewels of the Law" <note>original: Fah-Pao</note>. Monks are usually designated "Disciples of the Law" <note>original: Fah-Men</note>. The endless process of reincarnation is symbolized by the "Wheel of the Law" <note>original: Fah-Luen</note>. The spreading of Buddhist teachings is typified by "Turning the Wheel of the Law" <note>original: Chuan-Fah-Luen</note>. Religious names based on the concept of the Law are held in high regard among Buddhist monastic orders, such as "Lover of the Law" <note>original: Fah-Ai</note>, "Approved in the Law" <note>original: Fah-Lien</note>, and "Brightness of the Law" <note>original: Fah-Ming</note>. (Compare Eitel’s *Handbook of Chinese Buddhism*).</note>

<summary>The Buddha explains that an enlightened person must practice charity without being influenced by sensory perceptions or mental concepts to gain immeasurable merit. together, and were it possible for them to forget their sectarian subtilties and nice distinctions, they would find that there was a vast mass of speculation about the main principles of which they were agreed. They would be of one mind relative to the four following propositions: (1) That there is an objective potency of intellect; (2) That this potency can be rendered subjective by concentrated thought, ascetic exercises, or determined effort; (3) That this potency can only be acquired by the initiated; (4) That the initiated may enlarge this potency to a limitless extent. entity, a being, a living being, a personality, qualities or ideas coincident with <term>Law <note>original: "Dharma." Therefore, enlightened disciples ought not to affirm the permanency or reality of qualities or ideas coincident with Law, nor postulate as being of an inviolate nature, qualities or ideas having an existence apart from the concept of Law.

# THE DIAMOND SUTRA

minds in the simile of a raft <note>The Buddha famously compared his teachings to a raft used to cross a dangerous river. Once a traveler reaches the "other shore" (enlightenment), it would be foolish to continue carrying the heavy raft on their back. The teachings are a means to an end, not the end itself.</note>. If the <term>Law <note>original: "Dharma." In this context, it refers to the body of Buddhist teachings and the path toward spiritual truth.</note></term>—having fulfilled its function in bearing you to the other shore (<term>Nirvana <note>The state of final liberation from the cycle of suffering and rebirth.</note></term>)—with its coincident qualities and ideas must inevitably be abandoned, how much more inevitable must be the abandonment of qualities or ideas [that are not the Law]? <note>This completes the logic: if one must eventually let go of even the "good" teachings once enlightened, one must certainly let go of "bad" or incorrect views.</note>

The Lord Buddha addressed Subhuti, saying: "What think you? Has the Lord Buddha really attained to <term>supreme spiritual wisdom <note>original: "Anuttara Samyak Sambodhi." This refers to the highest, most complete enlightenment of a Buddha, which is beyond all comparison and conceptual thought.</note></term>? Subhuti replied, saying: "As I understand the meaning of the Lord Buddha's discourse, he has no system of doctrine which can be specifically formulated; nor can the Lord Buddha express, in explicit terms, a form of knowledge which can be described as supreme spiritual wisdom. Here referring to the Buddha's teachings and the underlying universal truth.</note></term>, is transcendental and inexpressible. Being a purely spiritual concept, it is neither consonant with Law, nor synonymous with anything apart from the Law. Thus<sup>1</sup> is exemplified

In Buddhist philosophy, this refers to the universal truth, the teachings of the Buddha, and the underlying order of the universe.</note> by which they attained to supreme spiritual wisdom <note>This translates the Sanskrit term *Anuttara Samyak Sambodhi*, meaning the complete, unexcelled enlightenment of a Buddha.</note>, each owe their beginning to the truth <note>The 1912 translator notes: "Faith is in the world the best property for a man; the Law <note>original: "Dhamma"</note>, well observed, conveys happiness; truth, indeed, is the sweetest of things; and the life they call the best which is lived with understanding." — From the *Sutta-Nipata*, a collection of the Buddha's discourses.</note> of this sacred Scripture. Subhuti, what is commonly called the Law of the Buddha <note>original: "Buddhic Law"</note>, is not actually a Law that belongs to the Buddha." <note>The translator provides an alternative reading: "What then, Subhuti? All the Buddhas, and all the perfect laws of the Buddhas, have sprung from the principles of this one <term>Sutra<note>A scriptural narrative or collection of teachings.</note></term>; but, Subhuti, that which is spoken of as the Law of Buddha, is after all not such a Law."</note>

<note>The translator includes an explanation of the Law from the scholar Spence Hardy: "The Law <note>original: "Dharmma"</note> is perfect, having nothing extra, and nothing wanting. But it requires attention, so that the benefits it offers may be received. Though the teacher may attain great happiness, and enter <term>Nirvana<note>The state of final liberation from the cycle of suffering and rebirth.</note></term>, it does not follow that the student will necessarily possess the same privileges; he may be like one who places a crown upon the head of another. Therefore each person for themselves must practice meditation and follow the rules, so that they may attain wisdom." — *Eastern Monachism*.</note>

<summary>The Buddha explains that all enlightened beings and their teachings arise from the truths in this Sutra, yet he warns that the "Law of Buddha" should not be viewed as a rigid or possessable doctrine.</summary>

The Lord Buddha addressed **Subhuti** <note>One of the Buddha's chief disciples, known for his profound understanding of "Emptiness" or the nature of reality.</note>, saying: "What do you think? When the Lord Buddha, in a previous life, was a disciple of the **Illuminating Buddha** <gloss>Dipankara Buddha</gloss> <note>original: "Dipankara Buddha"—a legendary Buddha of the distant past who predicted that the historical Buddha would eventually achieve enlightenment.</note>, was there any specific doctrine or law communicated to him?"

¹ "**The Lamp-Lighting Buddha** <gloss>Ran-Teng-Fuh</gloss> <note>original: "Ran-Teng-Fuh"</note>—the Buddha who illuminates brightly, or **The Buddha of Fixed Light** <gloss>Ting-Kwang-Fuh</gloss> <note>original: "Ting-Kwang-Fuh"</note>. He was the twenty-fourth predecessor of the historical Buddha, **The Sage of the Shakyas** <gloss>Sakyamuni</gloss> <note>original: "Sakyamuni"</note>, and it was from him that the latter received the assurance of his being destined for Buddhahood." — *Handbook of Chinese Buddhism*, **Ernest John Eitel**.

It is recorded in *The Diamond Sutra* that the Lord Buddha, in his previous incarnations, diligently performed religious vows and showed great deference and honor to all the Buddhas of those eras. An image of this former master, the **Illuminating Buddha** <gloss>Dipankara Buddha</gloss>, can frequently be seen in Chinese Buddhist temples, situated immediately behind the more prominent figure of the **Sage of the Shakyas** <gloss>Sakyamuni Buddha</gloss>. Among the annual Chinese Buddhist festivals is the birthday of "the ancient Buddha, **The Lamp-Lighter**" <gloss>Ran-Teng</gloss> <note>original: "Ran-Teng"</note>, and the time set aside for its celebration is the 22nd day of the 8th lunar month. (Compare with **Joseph Edkins'** *Chinese Buddhism*.)

# THE DIAMOND SUTRA

->47<-

known as *The Diamond Sutra*,¹ ‘The Transcendent Wisdom,’ by means of which we reach ‘The Other Shore’ <note>original: "Paramita"—a term meaning "perfection" or "reaching the opposite shore," symbolizing the transition from the world of suffering (Samsara) to enlightenment (Nirvana).</note>. By this name you shall reverently regard it! And why? Subhuti, what the Lord Buddha declared as ‘transcendent wisdom’ by means of which we reach ‘the other shore,’ is not essentially ‘transcendent wisdom’—in its essence it transcends all wisdom.” <note>This highlights a central theme of the Sutra: that any concept, even "wisdom" itself, is a temporary tool or "raft" and should not be clung to as an absolute reality.</note>

The Lord Buddha addressed Subhuti, saying:² “What think you? Did the Lord

¹ A Chinese annotator <note>This likely refers to traditional scholars such as Prince Zhaoming or the monk Hanshan, whose commentaries often accompanied the Chinese text of the Sutra.</note> observes, that as the “diamond” excels all other precious gems in brilliance and indestructibility, so also the “wisdom” of this Sutra transcends and shall out-live all other knowledge known to philosophy.

² “Then what do you think, O Subhuti, is there anything that was preached by the One Who Has Thus Gone <note>original: "Tathagata"—a title for the Buddha, meaning one who has arrived at or departed into the ultimate reality.</note>? Subhuti said: Not indeed, O Blessed One <note>original: "Bhagavat"—a term of address for the Buddha equivalent to "Lord" or "Adored One."</note>, there is nothing that was preached by the One Who Has Thus Gone.” —*The Diamond-Cutter* <note>original: "Vagrakkahedika"—the Sanskrit title of the Sutra is *Vajracchedika Prajnaparamita Sutra*.</note>. MAX MÜLLER. <note>Friedrich Max Müller was a famous 19th-century philologist; Gemmell is comparing his own translation with Müller's scholarly version.</note>

It appears to be one of the distinctive features of primitive Buddhism, that its founder made provision for the utmost development of the human intellect, within the spheres of religion and philosophy. According to the text of *The Diamond Sutra*, the Lord Buddha evidently disclaims any suggestion on his part to formulate a “precise system of Law or doctrine” corresponding to the idea of a *creed*. <note>In this context, Gemmell suggests that the Buddha encouraged personal realization over the blind acceptance of dogmatic statements.</note>

He uses his historical patience during a past execution as proof that he had already moved beyond the illusions of individual personality.</summary>

# THE DIAMOND SUTRA

The Lord Buddha, in declaring the "unreality of phenomena <note>Phenomena refers to the observable world and the forms we perceive, which appear real but lack permanent essence.</note>," also affirmed "that the whole realm of sentient life is ephemeral and illusory." <note>original: "Every form of phenomena is really *not* phenomena; every form of sentient life is in reality *not* sentient life."</note>

"Subhuti, the sayings of the Lord Buddha are true, credible, and immutable. His utterances are neither extravagant nor chimerical. Subhuti, the plane <note>The Buddhist term used here is <term>Fah</term> <note>Sanskrit: *Dharma*; in this context, it refers to the Law, the Truth, or the ultimate reality of the Buddha's teachings.</note>.</note> of thought to which the Lord Buddha attained, cannot be explained in terms synonymous with reality or non-reality." <note>The Buddha's realization occupies a "Middle Way" that cannot be captured by simple definitions of "existence" or "non-existence."</note>

"Subhuti, in the exercise of charity <note>Charity refers to *Dana*, the practice of selfless giving, which is a primary virtue for those seeking enlightenment.</note>, if the mind of an enlightened disciple is not independent of every Law <note>The "Law" here suggests mental attachments to specific religious rules or the concept of the deed itself.</note>, he is like unto a person having entered impenetrable darkness, and to whom every object is invisible. But an enlightened disciple, discharging the exercise of charity with a mind independent of every Law, is like unto a person having the power of vision, in the meridian glory of the sunlight, and to whom every object is visible." <note>This analogy suggests that clinging to spiritual rules creates a "darkness" of attachment, while true freedom of mind allows for clear, unhindered perception of the truth.</note>

"Subhuti, in future ages, if a good disciple, whether man or woman, rigorously studies and..."

<summary>The Buddha explains that his teachings are immutable and transcend the dualistic concepts of reality and non-reality. He uses a vivid analogy to show that practicing charity while attached to religious concepts is like walking in darkness, whereas practicing with a detached mind is like seeing in bright sunlight.</summary>

<note>The **omniscience** <note>The quality of having infinite knowledge or being all-knowing.</note> of Buddha is not the knowledge of all things, but the power of knowing whatever he wishes to know. In opposition to other teachers, who deduce their doctrines from certain previously assumed principles, and who may err either in the data, or in the deductions from them, Buddha affirms of himself that the complete field of truth is before him, that the eye of wisdom to perceive it was obtained by him when he became a Buddha; and whatever he desires to know he perceives perfectly, and at one glance, without any reasoning process. — (Rev. D. T. Gogerly, in the *Friend of Ceylon*. Quoted by R. Spence Hardy, in *Eastern Monachism*).</note>

# THE DIAMOND SUTRA

“Subhuti, if an enlightened disciple holds onto arbitrary ideas of <term>sentient life (beings capable of feeling and perception)</term> as a distinct entity, a soul, a living being, or a separate personality, he has not truly attained to <term>supreme spiritual wisdom (Anuttara Samyak Sambodhi, the perfect enlightenment of a Buddha)</term>. And why? Because, Subhuti, there is no fixed <term>Law (Dharma, or a set of defined rules)</term> by means of which a disciple may be defined as one having obtained supreme spiritual wisdom.”¹

¹ Most writers on the Buddhist faith and religion refer to the series of events which culminated in the Lord Buddha obtaining “supreme enlightenment.” The founder of the Buddhist faith, dissatisfied with the practice of <term>asceticism (the practice of severe self-discipline and abstaining from all forms of indulgence)</term>, and disappointed by his unfaithful disciples, walked meditatively towards the river <term>Nairanjara (a river in the Indian state of Bihar)</term>, where <term>Sujata (a village woman who offered the Buddha milk-rice to regain his strength)</term>, “the daughter of a neighboring villager,” provided him with his morning meal. Seating himself under a sacred <term>Bo-Tree (the Bodhi Tree, or ‘Tree of Awakening’)</term>, he immediately became engaged in the severest of mental conflicts. The Buddhist authors describe their Master as sitting “sublime,” “calm,” and “serene” throughout the sustained assault of a “visible” and wicked <term>tempter (Mara, the demon who personifies desire, death, and spiritual distraction)</term>, assisted by legions of evil spirits. So unrelenting was the fierce encounter, that the forces of nature shook and were convulsed under the dreadful onslaught. As the day advanced, the spiritual elements in Buddha’s nature gradually gained <note>The text here breaks off, but historical accounts conclude that by nightfall, Buddha achieved total insight and became the Awakened One.</note>

<summary>The Buddha explains that true enlightenment is incompatible with the belief in a permanent, individual self or soul. The accompanying note describes the historical and symbolic struggle of the Buddha under the Bo-Tree to achieve this state.</summary>

# THE DIAMOND SUTRA

The Lord Buddha addressed <term>Subhuti (a senior disciple known for his profound understanding of "Emptiness")</term>, saying: “What think you? When the Lord Buddha was a disciple of <term>Dipankara Buddha (the twenty-fourth predecessor of the historical Buddha, who prophesied his future enlightenment)</term>, was there bequeathed to him any <term>Law (Dharma; a fixed set of rules or ultimate truth)</term> whereby he attained to <term>supreme spiritual wisdom (Anuttara Samyak Sambodhi, the perfect, unexcelled awakening)</term>?”

Subhuti replied, saying: “No! <term>Honoured of the Worlds (a title of respect for the Buddha)</term>! Inasmuch as I am able to comprehend the meaning of the Lord Buddha’s discourse, when the Lord Buddha was a disciple of Dipankara Buddha, there was no Law bequeathed to him whereby he attained to supreme spiritual wisdom.”

...<note>This section completes the footnote from the previous page, describing the Buddha's mental victory over the tempter Mara.</note> the ascendency; and when he became “fully enlightened,” there was revealed to him an antidote for human woe. The mind of the Lord Buddha thereafter assumed an aspect of perfect peace; “*and in the power over the human heart of inward culture, and of love to others,*” the great Teacher discovered a foundation of Truth, where, with assurance of faith, he could securely rest. 

As <term>Milton (John Milton, the 17th-century English poet)</term> regarded “Paradise” to be “regained” in the wilderness, and not on <term>Calvary (the site of the crucifixion of Jesus)</term>; in like manner the Buddhist poets indicate a belief that the experience of their Master under the <term>Bo-Tree (the Bodhi Tree, or ‘Tree of Awakening’)</term> was the most eventful in his history. That is the reason they regard the Bo-Tree with a reverence resembling the Christian veneration of the Cross. (Compare <term>Davids’ *Buddhism* (a reference to T.W. Rhys Davids, a famous 19th-century scholar of Oriental studies)</term>.)

<summary>The Buddha explains that his enlightenment was not a "Law" or thing passed to him by his predecessor, Dipankara Buddha, but an internal realization. A commentary links the Buddha's moment of awakening to the poetic and spiritual milestones found in Western traditions.</summary>

# THE DIAMOND SUTRA

The Lord Buddha enquired of Subhuti, saying: “What do you think? Concerning the sands of the Ganges <note>The Ganges is the most sacred river of India; its vast banks provided the Buddha with a frequent metaphor for infinity.</note>, did the Lord Buddha declare that these were grains of sand?” Subhuti assenting, said: “Honoured of the Worlds! the Lord Buddha declared that these were grains of sand.”

The Lord Buddha enquired of Subhuti, saying: “What do you think? The Lord Buddha addressed Subhuti, saying: “What think you? If a disciple, having obtained all the treasures of this universe <note>The seven treasures: gold, silver, pearls, coral, cornelian, glass, and crystal, representing the height of worldly wealth.</note>, were to bestow these in the exercise of charity, would such a disciple consequently enjoy a considerable merit <note>merit: the spiritual credit or positive karma earned through good deeds that leads to better future circumstances.</note>?” Subhuti assenting, said: “Honoured of the Worlds <note>A title for the Buddha, signifying his universal respect across all realms of existence.</note>! such a disciple would consequently enjoy a very considerable merit.” <note>The logic here, as explained by the One Who Has Thus Come <note>original: “Tathagata” — a title the Buddha used for himself, meaning one who has arrived at or gone to the truth.</note>, is that what we call a "stock of merit" is not a permanent, physical thing. If merit were a tangible "stock" or "possession," it would not be true spiritual merit; it is called merit precisely because it is empty of a permanent self.</note>

<margin>Within the meaning of the Buddhist Law <note>original: "Buddhic Law"</note>, charity is purely a spiritual concept; and merit consequent upon fulfilling the Law of charity, must have a purely spiritual realisation. ¹ “The first of the Buddha’s bodies is the **Body of Reality** <note>original: "Dharma-Kaya" — in Buddhist theology, the ultimate, formless, and eternal essence of the universe.</note>, supposed to be a kind of ethereal essence of a highly sublimate... This essence was believed to be eternal, and after the Buddha’s death, was represented by the Law or doctrine <note>original: "Dharma"</note> he taught.

> “The second body is the **Body of Conscious Bliss** <note>original: "Sambho-ga-Kaya"</note>, which is of a less ethereal and more material nature than the last. # THE DIAMOND SUTRA

The Lord Buddha addressed Subhuti, saying: “Do not affirm that the Lord Buddha thinks thus within himself, ‘I ought to promulgate a system of <term>Law or doctrine<note>original: "Dharma." Here, it refers to the cosmic order and the body of teachings provided by the Buddha to lead others to enlightenment.</note>.’ Have no such irrelevant thought! And why? Because, if a disciple affirmed that the Lord Buddha promulgated a system of Law or doctrine, he would defame the Lord Buddha, being manifestly unable to understand the purport of my instruction. Subhuti, regarding the promulgation of a ‘system of Law or doctrine,’ there is in reality no ‘system of Law or doctrine’ to promulgate, it is merely termed a ‘system of Law or doctrine.’”¹

---

¹ “The <term>Blessed Lord<note>original: "Bhagavat," a title of supreme honor for the Buddha, often translated as "Lord" or "The Adorable One."</note></term> said : What do you think, O Subhuti, does the <term>One who has thus come<note>original: "Tathagata," the Buddha's preferred way of referring to himself, meaning one who has arrived at the truth of reality.</note></term> think in this wise : the <term>Law<note>original: "Dharma."</note></term> has been taught by me ? Because, O Subhuti, it is said the teaching of the Law, the teaching of the Law indeed, O Subhuti, there is <note>This footnote provides a comparison to another academic translation (likely Max Müller's) to show how different scholars handle these sacred technical terms. This noteworthy statement seems to <term>militate against<note>To act as a powerful factor against something; to conflict with.</note></term> some opinions expressed in Europe regarding the Buddhist theory of “matter.” According to our Chinese text, it does not appear that <term>Sakyamuni Buddha<note>original: "Shakyamuni." <meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Buddha and his disciple Subhuti. While the previous page addressed the nature of physical matter and "dust," this section shifts the focus to the psychological and spiritual concept of the "self" and the limitations of the human mind in grasping ultimate reality.</meta>

-># THE DIAMOND SUTRA<-

The Buddha expressed a concept<gloss>1</gloss> that the mind is able to understand the notion of an individual soul, a being, a living creature, or a distinct personality; what do you think, Subhuti, would that disciple be correctly interpreting my meaning?

> 1 “Because, O Subhuti, if a person were to say that a belief in self, a belief in a being, a belief in life, or a belief in personality had been taught by the <term>Tathagata<note>A title for the Buddha, often translated as "The One Who Has Thus Come" or "The One Who Has Thus Gone," signifying someone who has attained enlightenment and sees reality as it truly is.</note></term>, would they be speaking the truth? Subhuti replied, 'No, indeed, <term>Bhagavat<note>A Sanskrit term meaning "Blessed One" or "Lord," used as a respectful title for the Buddha.</note></term>, they would not be speaking the truth. And why? Because what was taught by the Tathagata as a "belief in self" was actually taught as "no-belief"; therefore, it is merely called "belief in self."'”—*The <term>Vagrakkhedika<note>original: "Vagrakkhedika." The Sanskrit title of the Diamond Sutra, which translates to "The Diamond Cutter" or "The Cutter of the Diamond."</note></term>.*
> **MAX MÜLLER.** <note>Friedrich Max Müller (1823–1900) was a renowned scholar and one of the founders of the Western academic study of Indian religions.</note>

These words illustrate another deep facet of Buddhist teaching. Beyond the intriguing questions regarding the existence of an individual soul, a being, a living creature, or a personality, another problem arises concerning our ability to fully grasp or "comprehend" these accepted abstract ideas. If we understand Buddhist doctrine correctly, these abstract concepts are composed of so many subtle spiritual elements that the human mind is incapable of breaking them down through any simple process of logic. In short—to the Buddhist mind, an individual soul, a being, a living creature, or a personality represents much more than what philosophy attempts to express in words.

<summary>This page discusses the Buddhist concept of "non-self," arguing that the human mind's labels for individuals—such as "personality" or "soul"—are abstract approximations that fail to capture a deeper, more complex spiritual reality.</summary>
<keywords>non-self, Subhuti, Tathagata, Max Müller, Buddhist psychology, personality, individual soul, Vagrakkhedika</keywords>
