...interpreting aright the meaning of my discourse?"

Subhuti replied, saying: "<term>Honoured of the Worlds<note>original: "Honoured of the Worlds!" A translation of the title "Lokajyestha," a respectful address to the Buddha acknowledging his supreme status among all beings.</note></term>! That disciple would not be interpreting the meaning of the Lord Buddha's discourse correctly. And why? Because, Honoured of the Worlds! when discoursing upon the comprehension of such ideas as an individual soul, a being, a living creature, and a distinct personality, it was declared that these are entirely unreal and illusive; therefore, they are merely termed an individual soul, a being, a living creature, and a personality." <note>Subhuti explains that these terms are labels used for convenience in speech, but they do not correspond to any permanent or independent reality.</note>

The Lord Buddha thereafter addressed Subhuti, saying: <gloss>1</gloss> "Those who aspire to the

---

<gloss>1</gloss> "In this way, O Subhuti, are all things to be perceived, to be looked upon, and to be believed by one who has entered upon the path of the <term>Bodhisattvas<note>Beings dedicated to attaining enlightenment for the benefit of all sentient beings.</note></term>. And in this wise are they to be perceived, to be looked upon, and to be believed—neither as the idea of a thing, nor as the idea of a 'no-thing.' And why? Because by saying 'the idea of a thing,' it has been preached by the <term>Tathagata<note>A title for the Buddha, meaning "One who has thus come" or "One who has thus gone," signifying his arrival at ultimate reality.</note></term> as the 'no-idea' of a thing." — *The <term>Diamond Cutter<note>original: "Vagrakkhedika." The Sanskrit title of this sutra.</note></term>.* **MAX MÜLLER.** <note>Friedrich Max Müller was a pioneering 19th-century scholar of Sanskrit and comparative religion.</note>

"Subhuti, the persons who aspire to the perfectly enlightened heart <note>Refers to 'Anuttara-samyak-sambodhi,' or the supreme, ultimate enlightenment of a Buddha.</note>, ought to know accordingly that this is true with respect to

<summary>Subhuti explains that concepts of the "self" and "personality" are merely linguistic labels for illusory states, and the Buddha introduces how a Bodhisattva should perceive the world without falling into the trap of fixed ideas.</summary>

Subhuti, concerning ‘visible objects,’ the Lord Buddha declared that these are not really ‘visible objects,’ they are merely termed ‘visible objects.’” <note>The Buddha is teaching that while we use names for things we see, these objects have no permanent, independent essence; they are "empty" of self-nature.</note>

The Lord Buddha addressed Subhuti, saying: “If a disciple, having immeasurable spheres filled with the seven treasures,<gloss>1</gloss> bestowed these in the exercise of charity; and if a disciple, whether man or woman, having aspired to supreme spiritual wisdom, selected from this Scripture a stanza comprising four lines, then rigorously observed <note>original: "four lines." In Buddhist tradition, a four-line stanza or "gatha" represents the essential core of a teaching. Reciting even a small portion is said to bring more merit than vast material wealth.</note>

all things, and thus prevent the exhibition of any characteristics on any point whatever. Subhuti, these very characteristics of which we speak are after all no characteristics, but a mere name.” — *Kin-Kong-King.<note>original: "Kin-Kong-King," the Chinese title (Jin gang jing) for the Diamond Sutra.</note>* BEAL.<note>This is a reference to the translation by Samuel Beal (1825–1889), a pioneer in translating Chinese Buddhist texts into English.</note>

"And in what way can the disciple 'proclaim them generally?' Simply by relying on no conditions or distinctions whatever; thus he will act without agitation or excitement. Wherefore the conclusion is this—that all things which admit of definition are as a dream, a phantom, a bubble, a shadow, as the dew and lightning flash. They ought to be regarded thus."—*The Diamond Sutra* <note>original: "Kin-Kong-King," the Chinese title for this scripture.</note>. BEAL.

"And how should he explain it? As in the sky: stars, darkness, a lamp, a phantom, dew, a bubble, a dream, a flash of lightning, and a cloud—thus should we look upon the world (all that was made)."—*The Diamond Cutter* <note>original: "The Vagrakkhedika," the Sanskrit title of the Diamond Sutra.</note>. When the Lord Buddha <term>Buddha: The "Awakened One," the title given to Siddhartha Gautama.</term> concluded his enunciation of this Scripture,¹ the venerable Subhuti <note>One of the Buddha's chief disciples, renowned for his profound understanding of the concept of "Emptiness."</note>, the monks <note>original: "bhikkhus"</note>, nuns <note>original: "bhikkhunis"</note>, lay-brethren and sisters, all mortals, and the whole realm of spiritual beings <note>This refers to various classes of beings in Buddhist cosmology, including gods (devas) and spirits (asuras).</note>, rejoiced exceedingly, and consecrated to its practice, they received it and departed.
